# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score += 1 for every **stationary** robot → robots should **MOVE**
- After switch time: score += 1 for every **moving** robot → robots should **STOP**
- Switch happens between step 400-600; at switch time a **GREEN wall** lights up
- Robots can detect the green color with their camera (C=7, color 2=green)

**Key insight:** 
- Before green appears: robots should be moving (Exploration/Attraction/Repulsion)
- After green appears: robots should stop (Stop behavior)
- The green wall is the signal to transition from moving to stopping

**Design strategy:**
- State 0: Move (Exploration) → transition to Stop when green is perceived
- State 1: Stop → maybe transition back if needed, but likely stay stopped

**FSM designs:**

1. **Simple 2-state**: Move → Stop on green detection
2. **2-state with probability**: Move → Stop on green (high prob), Stop → Move on no-green (low prob to handle edge cases)
3. **3-state**: Move → Stop on green, with intermediate attraction state
4. Various parameter variations

Let me design 14 machines:

- Machines 1-2: Elite/best guesses (2-state: explore → stop on green)
- Machines 3-7: Exploitations (vary parameters)
- Machines 8-14: Explorations (different topologies)

For the green color: clr=2 (green), C=7 condition with --l<I>x<K> 2

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and diversify around the most logical designs.

## Reasoning

**Core logic recap:**
- Before green wall appears: robots must MOVE (minimize stationary robots)
- After green wall appears: robots must STOP (minimize moving robots)
- Green wall (color 2) is the only signal available to distinguish phases

**Best expected design:** 
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green detected (high probability)
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped (very low probability of leaving)

**Key parameters to tune:**
- Detection probability for green (C=7, l=2): higher = faster response to green signal
- Exploration turning rate: affects coverage but not score directly
- Small leak probability from Stop state: should be near 0

**Improvements to try:**
- Use p=1.0 on green detection for instant response
- GoToColor green as intermediate (helps spread green signal info)
- Attraction to cluster robots (shared sensing of green)
- Multiple transitions to be robust

**Elitism:** Keep the best-looking designs (2-state: explore→stop on green with p=1.0 or p=0.95)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received explicit score feedback, but I can reason about what should work best and diversify systematically.

## Reasoning

**Mission analysis (re-confirmed):**
- Phase 1 (before green): robots must MOVE → minimize stationary robots
- Phase 2 (after green): robots must STOP → minimize moving robots
- Green wall is the ONLY reliable signal

**Core best design** should be:
- State 0: Exploration → go to Stop when green seen (p=1.0)
- State 1: Stop → stay (near-zero leak)

**Problem with simple 2-state:** Not all robots may see the green wall directly. Robots near the wall see it; others don't. Need a way to propagate the "stop" signal.

**Signal propagation idea:** If robots that see green STOP, then other robots noticing that their neighbours have stopped (neighbour count drops below threshold as moving robots spread out) could also stop. OR: robots seeing green could emit a signal via LED color that others detect... but we only have floor color and camera for perception.

**Better approach:** Use GoToColor(green) as an intermediate - robots that see green move toward it, which clusters them, and then they stop. But GoToColor keeps them moving...

**Attraction approach:** Robots move freely; when they see green, they stop. Other robots attracted to stopped clusters might also stop near them... but attraction just moves toward neighbors.

**LED signaling:** Robots that see green could set LED to a distinctive color (e.g., cyan=6), others could detect that color and also stop. This is the propagation mechanism!

Let me design machines with LED-based signal propagation:
- State 0: Explore, LED=off → if see green (C=7,l=2) → State 1
- State 1: Stop, LED=cyan(6) → stay stopped
- Other robots: if they see cyan (C=7,l=6) → also go to State 1

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

Since I'm not receiving explicit score feedback, I need to reason carefully about what's most likely working and what isn't.

**Critical re-analysis:**

The LED propagation idea is clever but has a flaw: robots need to be within 0.5m range to see each other's LEDs. If robots are spread out exploring, they may not be close enough to propagate the signal. 

**What's most likely scoring well:**
- Simple 2-state: Explore → Stop on green (p=1.0), Stop stays stopped
- The simpler the better - fewer failure modes

**Key issue I may have overlooked:** The problem says robots perceive neighbours through range-and-bearing (0.5m). The camera perceives "coloured objects" - this likely includes both robot LEDs AND the coloured wall. So green wall detection (C=7, l=2) should work for robots near the green wall.

**But wait** - if robots near the green wall stop, and robots far away keep moving (because they can't see the green wall), the score for phase 2 is still high. We need ALL robots to eventually stop.

**Best propagation strategy:**
1. Robots see green → stop, turn LED cyan
2. Robots see cyan LED (from stopped robots) → also stop, turn LED cyan
3. This cascades across the swarm

**Also worth trying:** Attraction behavior in phase 1 keeps robots clustered → more likely multiple robots see green simultaneously → faster propagation.

**Elitism:** Keep the best designs from rounds 2-3 (2-state with cyan LED propagation, p=1.0 on green).

**New explorations:**
- 3-state with clustering before green, then stop on green
- Vary the cyan detection probability
- Try magenta (5) as signal color too

# ROUND 6/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 5 rounds without score feedback, I need to think more carefully about what might be going wrong with my designs.

**Fundamental reconsideration:**

The camera in ARGoS e-puck robots perceives colored *objects* in the environment. The green wall is a physical colored block. Robot LEDs are also perceived by the camera. So C=7 with l=2 (green) should detect BOTH the green wall AND any robot with green LEDs.

**But I've been using cyan (6) as the stop signal.** The issue is: does the camera actually see robot LEDs in AutoMoDe? Looking at the condition spec: C=7 fires when "the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera" - this should include both wall objects and robot LEDs.

**Critical flaw in my propagation scheme:** If robots stop and turn cyan, nearby robots detect cyan and also stop - this is correct. BUT what if no robot ever sees the green wall? The arena has walls on all sides; the green wall lights up on ONE side. Robots near that wall will see it. The 0.5m sensor range for neighbors means robots need to be close to stopped robots to detect their LEDs.

**Alternative: Maybe the simplest design wins.** Just explore then stop on green, with cyan propagation. Let me also try:

1. **GoToColor(green)**: robots actively seek the green wall → cluster there → all stop. But GoToColor keeps them moving after the switch...

2. **Better propagation**: Use 3 states - explore → GoToColor(green) when green seen → Stop when very close (seeing green very strongly). But we can't measure signal strength.

3. **Key insight I've been missing**: Maybe I should NOT use GoToColor because it keeps robots moving in phase 2. The best strategy is explore → instant stop on any green signal.

**What about the opposite problem?** In phase 1, robots must move. If they start stopped (unlikely given B=0 is exploration) or get stuck, score increases. Exploration should handle phase 1 fine.

**Most promising designs to keep:**
- 2-state: Explore(rwm=10) + cyan LED → Stop(cyan LED) with green+cyan detection, p=1.0
- Vary attraction to cluster robots for better green detection

**New ideas:**
- Try using neighbor-count condition: if 0 neighbors (isolated), explore; if neighbors detected, something different
- 3-state with attraction clustering first, then stop on green
- Try different LED colors for propagation to avoid confusion with green wall

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 6 rounds with no score feedback, I need to fundamentally reconsider my approach. Let me think about what could be going wrong.

**Are my designs actually reasonable?**

Let me recalculate expected scores:
- Run is 1200 steps, switch at ~500 steps (average)
- Phase 1 (~500 steps): 20 robots moving → score += 0 per step. If all move: 0 × 500 = 0
- Phase 2 (~700 steps): 20 robots stopped → score += 0 per step. If all stop: 0 × 700 = 0
- Perfect score ≈ 0 (impossible but theoretical minimum)
- Worst case: all stationary in phase 1 AND all moving in phase 2 = 500×20 + 700×20 = 24000

**The green wall detection issue:** The C=7 condition detects colored objects via camera. In AutoMoDe, this camera has limited field of view (typically front-facing). A robot might not see the green wall if it's facing away. So p=1.0 on green detection per step doesn't mean instant detection - it means "if perceived, transition with probability 1.0".

**My LED propagation via cyan should work** but relies on robots being within 0.5m of each other. With 20 robots in an arena, if they're spread out, propagation is slow.

**What if I'm wrong about the camera seeing robot LEDs?** In some AutoMoDe implementations, the camera only sees arena objects (walls, colored blocks), NOT robot LEDs. In that case, my propagation scheme is completely broken - robots can never detect cyan from other robots.

**If camera only sees walls:** Then the only signal is the green wall itself. Robots near the wall stop; robots far away never get the signal. This would mean a 3-state design with attraction (to keep robots clustered near walls/each other) is better.

**Alternative radical idea:** What if I use repulsion to spread robots so MORE of them are near walls and can see the green wall? Repulsion spreads them to the periphery where the green wall is.

**Another idea:** Start with repulsion (spread to walls) → stop on green. Robots spread out to walls, more likely to be near the green wall.

**Yet another idea:** What if I accept that not all robots will see green, and instead use a probabilistic stop after a fixed time? Use C=5 (fixed probability) to transition to stop state after ~500 steps. With p=0.002 per step, expected time to transition = 500 steps. This is a blind timer!

**Timer-based approach:** 
- State 0: Explore with p_stop ≈ 0.002/step → after ~500 steps average, transitions to Stop
- This matches the expected switch time!
- Combined with green detection: whichever comes first

Let me try both approaches and combinations:

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 7 rounds with no score feedback, I'm flying blind. Let me do a deep rethink.

**What's the theoretical optimum?**
- Switch at step S (uniform 400-600, mean 500)
- Phase 1: S steps × (robots stationary) penalized
- Phase 2: (1200-S) steps × (robots moving) penalized
- If all robots move in phase 1 AND all stop in phase 2: score = 0

**The fundamental problem:** Without knowing when the switch happens, robots need a signal. The GREEN wall is that signal. But:
1. Not all robots may face/see the green wall
2. Camera has limited field of view in e-puck (front sector ~180°)
3. The green wall is only on ONE of potentially 4 walls

**What if my green detection is wrong?** In AutoMoDe, color=2 is GREEN. But what if the green wall uses a different color ID? Let me check: {1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan}. Green wall should be color 2. This seems right.

**Critical insight I've been missing:** Maybe I should try ALL color values for the wall detection, in case the mapping is different.

**Also: What about the floor?** The problem says floor is uniformly GRAY. So C=1 (gray floor) is always true - useless for switching. C=0 (black) and C=2 (white) would never fire.

**Timer-based fallback:** Since switch happens at 400-600 steps, a robot that starts moving and then transitions to stop with probability p per step will on average stop after 1/p steps. Setting p=0.002 gives mean 500 steps - perfect! This is a blind timer that statistically matches the switch time.

**Combined approach:** Green detection OR timer - whichever fires first.

**But wait - I should also try simply: what if robots just STOP immediately?** If score is minimized, and phase 2 is longer (700 steps vs 500), stopping immediately gives: 500×20 (phase 1 penalty) + 0×700 = 10000. Moving always gives: 0 + 700×20 = 14000. So STOPPING immediately is actually BETTER than moving always! The optimal is to move for ~500 steps then stop.

**Pure timer is likely competitive.** Let me focus heavily on timer-based transitions with various p values, combined with green detection.

**Also try: start stopped, move briefly, stop again** - no, that's worse.

**Best p values for timer:** 
- p=0.002: mean 500 steps (matches switch mean)
- p=0.0025: mean 400 steps (matches switch minimum)  
- p=0.00167: mean 600 steps (matches switch maximum)
- p=0.003: mean 333 steps (too early)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds with no score feedback, I need to be more systematic. Let me reason from scratch about what's most likely to work.

**Score calculation revisited:**
- Switch at step S ~ U[400,600], mean=500
- Phase 1: S steps, penalty for stationary robots
- Phase 2: (1200-S) steps, penalty for moving robots
- Expected score if always moving: 20 × (1200-500) = 14000
- Expected score if always stopped: 20 × 500 = 10000
- **Always stopped is better than always moving!**
- Optimal: move for exactly S steps then stop = 0

**This means even a simple "stop immediately" FSM scores ~10000, while "explore forever" scores ~14000.**

**Timer analysis:** If robot transitions from explore to stop at rate p per step:
- Expected transition time = 1/p
- Score = 20 × E[max(0, S - transition_time)] × (phase1 penalty) + 20 × E[max(0, transition_time - S)] × (phase2 penalty)
- Optimal timer: 1/p = 500, so p = 0.002

**But the green wall signal should do much better** if it works - it fires at exactly step S (when green appears), giving score ≈ 0 for all robots near the wall.

**Problem: robots far from the green wall.** The arena has 4 walls. Green appears on 1 wall. Robots near opposite wall may never see it.

**Solution I haven't tried: GoAwayColor(green).** Wait, that moves away from green. What about having robots explore and then when they DON'T see green but their timer fires, they stop?

**Better solution: Combine green detection + timer.** Already tried. Let me tune p more carefully.

**What p minimizes score with timer?**
- If p too small: robots stop too late (phase 2 penalty high)
- If p too large: robots stop too early (phase 1 penalty high)
- Switch at S~U[400,600]: optimal is to match the distribution

**Key new idea: Two-timer approach.**
- State 0: Explore → transition to Stop (p=0.002, mean 500 steps) OR on green (p=1.0)
- State 1: Stop (cle=6, propagate) → can't go back

**Another idea: What if I use neighbor-count to detect when others stop?**
When the green wall appears, some robots stop. Remaining robots notice fewer moving neighbors (but they can't directly count stopped robots... or can they? C=4 fires when neighbors BELOW threshold. If robots stop and leave range-and-bearing range... no, stopped robots are still there.)

Actually, C=3/C=4 count robots within 0.5m. Stopped robots are still counted. So neighbor count doesn't help detect who stopped.

**Let me try very different p values and also try the "just stop" baseline:**

FSM: --nstates 1 --s0 1 --cle0 0 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 5 --p0x0 0.001

Wait - 1 state can't transition to itself. And with 1 state, --n0x0 would point to state 0 (itself) which is invalid. Let me use 2 states where state 1 is stop and state 0 is also stop but with tiny chance of moving:

Actually let me just focus on what I believe works: explore + timer + green detection. Vary p carefully.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I still have no score feedback, so I need to commit to my best reasoning.

**Final analysis of what should work:**

The mission requires:
1. Move before green wall appears (~500 steps)
2. Stop after green wall appears

**Key timer insight:** p=0.002 gives mean stop time of 500 steps, matching the switch perfectly. This is a pure timer baseline that should score reasonably well even without green detection.

**Green detection combined with timer:** Best of both worlds - stop immediately when green seen, or fall back to timer.

**LED propagation (cyan):** Robots that see green stop and emit cyan. Others detect cyan and also stop. This helps propagate the signal.

**What I should NOT do:** Overly complex 3-4 state machines that add failure modes.

**Final commitment:** Focus on the core 2-state machines with:
1. Green detection (p=1.0)
2. Cyan LED propagation 
3. Timer fallback (p=0.002 to 0.0025)

Also include pure timer variants as robust fallbacks.

For the final round, I'll submit the 12 most promising variants, heavily focused on the timer+green+cyan combination with slight parameter variations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
